- Addressing Cascading Failures: https://sre.google/sre-book/addressing-cascading-failures/
- (adjacent, surfaced) Production Service Best Practices: https://sre.google/sre-book/service-best-practices/
- (adjacent) https://sre.google/resources/book-update/handling-overload/

**DOĞRULANMADI — I could not fetch any sre.google page.** The fetch tool refused the domain ("Unable to verify if domain sre.google is safe to fetch"), and the same for `landing.google.com`, `google.github.io`, and `web.archive.org` mirrors. Everything below is from search-result summaries only, not from the primary page. **Do not quote these as verbatim.**

- Criticality levels are named CRITICAL_PLUS, CRITICAL, SHEDDABLE_PLUS, SHEDDABLE. Prescription reported: an overloaded task rejects requests of lower criticality first; criticality propagates automatically through the RPC chain; the adaptive-throttling stats are kept **per criticality**. DOĞRULANMADI (search summary).
- Client-side / adaptive throttling: when a client sees a significant portion of recent requests rejected with "out of quota", it self-regulates and caps its own outgoing traffic; "requests above the cap fail locally without even reaching the network." This is the closest SRE-book analogue to a client-side budget kill switch — the caller stops spending before the call leaves the process. DOĞRULANMADI.
- Load Shedding and Graceful Degradation (section exists in both chapters): prescription reported as serving "lower-quality, cheaper-to-compute results", and when even degraded responses are too expensive for all queries, "graceful load shedding, using well-behaved queuing and dynamic timeouts." DOĞRULANMADI.
- Testing the degraded path — two reported prescriptions, both directly relevant: (a) load-test to the capacity limit AND **test the failure mode for overload**, in a realistic environment; (b) "Test Noncritical Backends" — verify that unavailability of a noncritical backend does not interfere with critical components, because requests can slow down and consume resources waiting on a noncritical backend. DOĞRULANMADI.
- "Immediate Steps to Address Cascading Failures" section exists but I could not retrieve its item list. DOĞRULANMADI.

## 3. AWS Well-Architected

**REL05-BP01 Implement graceful degradation to transform applicable hard dependencies into soft dependencies** — https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/reliability-pillar/rel_mitigate_interaction_failure_graceful_degradation.html (fetched verbatim)
- Statement: "Application components should continue to perform their core function even if dependencies become unavailable. They might be serving slightly stale data, alternate data, or even no data."
- Desired outcome: "Failure modes of components should be seen as normal operation."
- Risk if not established: **High**.
- Anti-pattern named that maps 1:1 to a badly-built kill switch: "**Not testing that components are functional even during dependency failures**" and "Serving no data on errors ... when partial results can still be returned."
- The design prescription for a budget switch is here: "For each failure mode, have a way to deliver most or at least the most critical functionality... These considerations can become **additional requirements that can be tested and verified**."
- Circuit breaker is prescribed for the overloaded-downstream case: "If requests to a downstream system are consistently failing, it does not make sense to continue retrying... The circuit breaker pattern can be utilized here."
- Parameter-store degradation — directly applicable to "what if the flag backend is down": "To transform a parameter store, soft dependency caching or **sane defaults included in container or machine images** may be used. Note that **these defaults need to be kept up-to-date and included in test suites**."
- Critical caveat: "the pathways taken in case of component failure need to be tested and should be **significantly simpler than the primary pathway**. Generally, fallback strategies should be avoided" (links https://aws.amazon.com/builders-library/avoiding-fallback-in-distributed-systems/).

**REL05-BP02 Throttle requests** — https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/reliability-pillar/rel_mitigate_interaction_failure_throttle_requests.html (verbatim)
- "Requests below throttling rates are processed while those over the defined limit are rejected with a return message indicating the request was throttled." Risk if not established: High.
- Prescribed mechanism: "consider implementing the **token bucket algorithm**, where a token counts for a request. Tokens are refilled at a throttle rate per second and emptied asynchronously by one token per request." Return `429 Too Many Requests`.
- Testing prescriptions that apply to a budget cap: "Test limits that you plan to set... and document the tested limits" and "**Do not increase limits beyond what you establish in testing.**"
- Anti-pattern: "API endpoint throttles are not implemented or are left at default values without considering expected volumes" and "throttling limits are not tested."

**REL05-BP04 Fail fast and limit queues** — https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/reliability-pillar/rel_mitigate_interaction_failure_fail_fast.html (verbatim)
- "When a service is unable to respond successfully to a request, **fail fast**. This allows resources associated with a request to be released, and permits a service to recover if it's running out of resources." Risk: High.
- Anti-pattern relevant to a kill switch that silently swallows: "Not validating inputs or **using assertions to implement fail fast mechanisms** in software that bubble up exceptions to higher level components that can handle errors gracefully."
- Prescribes: "Implement programmatic assertions or specific metrics in your software and use them to **explicitly alert on system issues**"; "Use CloudWatch metrics and alarms to fail away from impaired resources."
- Also cites Fowler's own https://www.martinfowler.com/ieeeSoftware/failFast.pdf.
- REL05-BP03 Control and limit retry calls exists at https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/reliability-pillar/rel_mitigate_interaction_failure_limit_retries.html (not fetched).

**COST pillar — COST02-BP05 Implement cost controls** — https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/cost-optimization-pillar/cost_govern_usage_controls.html (mirror: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/framework/cost_govern_usage_controls.html)
DOĞRULANMADI as verbatim — the fetch tool intermittently refused docs.aws.amazon.com on the COST pages after succeeding on the REL pages. Content below is from search summaries of those exact URLs:
- Prescribed sequence is three-tiered: (1) **notify on spend** — "A common first step in implementing cost controls is to set up notifications when cost or usage events occur outside of the organization policies"; AWS Budgets at aggregate and granular level (linked account, service, tag, AZ); one budget per account plus finer budgets inside each account. (2) **automated enforcement** — "AWS Budget Actions ... can enforce specific IAM or SCP policies, or **stop target Amazon EC2 or Amazon RDS instances**, and Budget Actions can be started automatically or require workflow approval." This is AWS's canonical budget-threshold-triggers-a-shutoff pattern. (3) **anomaly detection** — AWS Cost Anomaly Detection, ML-based, for unusual spend.
- The soft-cap/hard-cap framing appeared in the search summary but I could not attribute it to the AWS page itself. **DOĞRULANMADI, likely not AWS wording.**
- COST02-BP06 Track project lifecycle: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/cost-optimization-pillar/cost_govern_usage_track_lifecycle.html — not fetched, DOĞRULANMADI.
- COST 2 question hub: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/en_us/wellarchitected/latest/framework/cost-02.html ; COST01-BP05 Report and notify on cost optimization: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/framework/cost_cloud_financial_management_usage_report.html
- Also surfaced: AWS DevOps Guidance [AG.ACG.7] "Use automated tools for scalable cost management" — https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/devops-guidance/ag.acg.7-use-automated-tools-for-scalable-cost-management.html (not fetched).

## 4. LaunchDarkly

**Kill switch flags** — https://launchdarkly.com/docs/home/flags/killswitch (fetched)
- "Kill switch flags, sometimes called **circuit breaker flags**, are **permanent boolean flags** with two variations: 'Enabled' (true) and 'Disabled' (false)." Note LD collapses the distinction Fowler keeps — LD calls the manual switch a "circuit breaker flag."
- Purpose: "permanent safety mechanisms" to "turn off **non-core functionality or third-party tools** in an emergency." (An LLM call is exactly this shape.)
- Targeting prescription: "Because kill switch or circuit breaker flags are used to shut off functionality or third-party tools completely, they **typically do not use complex targeting rules**. Instead, they serve 'Enabled' by default when targeting is on, and 'Disabled' when targeting is off." → the kill switch should be a plain on/off, not a segmented rollout.

**Flag conventions** — https://launchdarkly.com/docs/guides/flags/flag-conventions (fetched)
- Naming rule: "Flag names should read as an instructional sentence that begins with an **action** and concludes with a **subject**" (e.g. "Release:", "Configure:" + "Widget"). Reader should know the controlled behavior from the name alone.
- Temporary vs permanent: "After you verify the new code is stable and roll out the feature to 100% of customers, you should **delete the temporary flag**." Versus: "Operational flags control or change how your app or service operates" and "**are usually permanent**."
- Concrete kill-switch naming example given: `kill-switch-disable-acme-integration`.
- Operational show flags "must be **side effect-free and safe to turn on and off for any end user**." — the strongest operational prescription for a budget switch: flipping it must not corrupt state.
- Prerequisites: LD documents prerequisite flags mainly on **migration flags**, "used primarily as a prerequisite in other flags to facilitate coordination between work groups" — i.e. a parent flag gates dependent flags. (Prerequisites are not documented as the kill-switch mechanism itself.)

**Automating the flip from a monitoring threshold — Flag triggers**: https://launchdarkly.com/docs/home/releases/triggers , manage: https://launchdarkly.com/docs/home/releases/triggers-manage , partner capability: https://docs.launchdarkly.com/integrations/partner-integrations/triggers , Dynatrace guide: https://docs.launchdarkly.com/guides/integrations/triggers-dynatrace , video tutorial: https://launchdarkly.com/docs/tutorials/videos/kill-switch
- Reported: triggers create a **unique unguessable webhook URL** per flag; any tool that fires a webhook (APM, CI) can set targeting On/Off; built-in Datadog and Honeycomb integrations; Enterprise plan. DOĞRULANMADI (search summary — these pages were not fetched).
- Also reported from the operational-flags blog: "Put processes and controls in place to prevent operational flags from accidentally being flipped or deleted"; use tags + custom roles/RBAC to control who can toggle or delete a flag per environment. Source is https://launchdarkly.com/blog/operational-flags-best-practices/ — **blog, not docs; DOĞRULANMADI**.

## 5. OpenFeature (CNCF)

**Does it document a kill-switch pattern?** No — there is no kill-switch-named pattern in the spec. Its value here is entirely the **failure semantics**: what your code returns when the flag backend is down.

**Flag Evaluation API spec** — https://openfeature.dev/specification/sections/flag-evaluation/ (fetched)
- **Requirement 1.4.10: "Flag evaluation calls must always return the `default value` in the event of abnormal execution."** This is the load-bearing guarantee: a provider outage never throws into your call path, it returns your hardcoded default.
- Requirement 1.3.4: a type mismatch from the provider "is to be considered abnormal execution, and the supplied `default value` should be returned."
- Requirement 1.4.8: on abnormal execution the evaluation details' error code field MUST be populated.
- No-op provider: "in the absence of a configured provider the `evaluation API` uses the 'No-op provider', which simply returns the supplied default flag value."

**Design consequence for a budget kill switch:** the default value must be chosen so that *flag-backend-down = safe*. If the flag is `enable_llm_call` defaulting to `true`, a backend outage re-enables the expensive path. Inverting to `disable_llm_call` (default `true`) or `enable_llm_call` (default `false`) makes the outage fail closed. The spec guarantees the default is returned; it does not tell you which polarity to pick.

**Error codes / reasons** — https://openfeature.dev/specification/types/ (fetched verbatim)
- Error codes: `PROVIDER_NOT_READY` ("The value was resolved before the provider was initialized"), `FLAG_NOT_FOUND`, `PARSE_ERROR`, `TYPE_MISMATCH`, `TARGETING_KEY_MISSING`, `INVALID_CONTEXT`, `PROVIDER_FATAL` ("The provider has entered an irrecoverable error state"), `GENERAL`.
- Resolution reasons include `STATIC`, `DEFAULT` ("The resolved value fell back to a pre-configured value"), `ERROR` ("The resolved value was the result of an error"), plus `TARGETING_MATCH`, `SPLIT`, `CACHED`, `DISABLED`, `UNKNOWN`, `STALE`. → you can distinguish "flag was off" (`DISABLED`/`TARGETING_MATCH`) from "backend broke and you got the default" (`ERROR`), which is what you must log/alarm on.

**Provider concept** — https://openfeature.dev/docs/reference/concepts/provider/ (fetched)
- "Providers are responsible for performing flag evaluations. They provide an abstraction between the underlying flag management system and the OpenFeature SDK." Benefit: "the underlying flag evaluation logic to be changed without requiring a major code refactor."
- "If no provider is set, OpenFeature will no-op and return the default value passed to the evaluation API."
- "Registering an additional provider will override any previously configured providers" — one provider per domain, change is immediate for existing clients.
- Other spec sections: https://openfeature.dev/specification/sections/providers/ , hooks https://openfeature.dev/specification/sections/hooks/ , appendix A https://openfeature.dev/specification/appendix-a/

## 6. Microsoft — Circuit Breaker
URL: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/architecture/patterns/circuit-breaker (fetched verbatim, page dated ms.date 2025-02-05)

- Intent: "Temporarily block access to a remote service or resource **after failures reach a threshold**, instead of repeatedly retrying an operation that's likely to fail."
- States: **Closed** (count failures; "If the number of recent failures exceeds a specified threshold within a given time period, the proxy is placed into the Open state"), **Open** ("The request from the application fails immediately"), **Half-Open** (limited trial requests; success → Closed, any failure → Open).
- **Does it distinguish circuit breaker from kill switch? Not by name — but it documents the manual variant as a sub-feature:** under "Problems and considerations", **"Manual override:** If the recovery time for a failing operation is extremely variable, you should provide a **manual reset** option that enables an administrator to close a circuit breaker and reset the failure counter. Similarly, **an administrator can force a circuit breaker into the Open state** and restart the time-out timer if the protected operation is temporarily unavailable." That forced-Open is precisely the kill switch, expressed as an operator override on an automatic breaker.
- Open state need not throw: "In some cases, rather than returning a failure and raising an exception, the **Open state can return a default value** that's meaningful to the application."
- **The page's worked Example is essentially a cost/quota kill switch** — it uses the Azure Cosmos DB free tier: on the first `429`, the breaker trips Open, "returns default or cached responses and informs users of temporary degradation", Azure Monitor dynamic-threshold alerts notify ops, who may scale up or wait. The stated goal: "workload teams can manage cost increases deliberately and maintain service quality **without unexpectedly increasing operating expenses**."
- Explicitly separates it from Retry: "The Retry pattern enables an application to retry an operation with the expectation that it eventually succeeds. The Circuit Breaker pattern prevents an application from performing an operation that's likely to fail."
- Maps to WAF pillars: "Use this pattern to trigger **graceful degradation** in the workload" (RE:03, RE:07 Self-preservation, PE:07, PE:11).

## DUMP — things I found that you didn't ask for, and things I couldn't access

**Not asked, but load-bearing:**
1. **Fowler's own rule forbids the inline `if` for a kill switch.** "Conditionals are not advised... where you expect the Toggle Point to be long-lived" — and kill switches are the one toggle type he calls long-lived. If the implementation is `if (os.getenv("LLM_ENABLED"))` sprinkled at call sites, it violates the article on two axes at once (scattering + long-lived conditional), and the env var itself is his slowest-to-flip tier. If the claim being made is "we follow Fowler", the env-var-only version is the weakest defensible form.
2. **AWS explicitly warns against fallbacks in general** ("Generally, fallback strategies should be avoided", linking the Builders' Library article https://aws.amazon.com/builders-library/avoiding-fallback-in-distributed-systems/) while prescribing graceful degradation. The reconciliation AWS gives: the degraded path "should be **significantly simpler than the primary pathway**" and must be tested. A degraded path with its own complexity is an anti-pattern by AWS's own text.
3. **The strongest testing mandate is AWS's, not Google's** — REL05-BP01 lists "Not testing that components are functional even during dependency failures" as a named common anti-pattern at High risk, and says defaults "need to be kept up-to-date and **included in test suites**." That is a citable requirement for a test that runs with the kill switch flipped.
4. **Microsoft's circuit-breaker example is a cost-control example, not a reliability example.** That is unusually directly on-topic for "shut off an expensive path when a threshold is hit" and is probably a better citation than the AWS COST pillar for the *code-level* pattern.
5. **AWS Budgets Actions is the only source found that prescribes an automated mechanism actually wired to a spend threshold** (stop instances / attach SCP or IAM policy, auto or approval-gated). Everything else in the corpus triggers on *failure/load*, not on *cost*. There is a real gap: no primary source found prescribes "LLM token budget exceeded → flip flag." LaunchDarkly flag triggers + a cost alarm webhook is the closest composable answer, and I found it stated on the LD blog rather than in a doc that I fetched.
6. **OpenFeature's `DISABLED` vs `ERROR` reason codes** give you a free observability distinction: whether the expensive path was skipped deliberately or skipped because the flag system broke. Worth logging separately; nobody asked but it's the difference between "budget guard worked" and "guard was blind."
7. Fowler's Knight Capital reference ($460M) is his stated evidence that stale/mismanaged flags are themselves a production risk — relevant if a permanent kill switch is added and then forgotten.
8. LaunchDarkly's "operational flags must be **side effect-free and safe to turn on and off for any end user**" is the cleanest one-line acceptance criterion for a kill switch, and it's in docs, not blog.

**Could not access / unverified:**
- **Entire Google SRE book. `sre.google` is blocked at the domain level for the fetch tool**, as are `landing.google.com`, `google.github.io`, and `web.archive.org`. All SRE claims above are search-summary derived and marked DOĞRULANMADI. I do not have verbatim text for criticality level definitions, the adaptive throttling formula (the `max(0, (requests - K*accepts)/(requests+1))` form is well known but I could NOT verify it from the page), the "Load Shedding and Graceful Degradation" section, or "Immediate Steps to Address Cascading Failures". If any of this is going into a public claim, it must be re-fetched from a machine that can reach sre.google, or cited from the O'Reilly print edition.
- **AWS COST pillar pages (COST02-BP05, COST02-BP06)** — fetch refused `docs.aws.amazon.com` on the second and third rounds after the REL pages succeeded, so the block appears rate-based/intermittent rather than permanent. Retrying later will likely work. COST02-BP06 "Track project lifecycle" is entirely unread.
- LaunchDarkly flag-triggers docs pages, REL05-BP03, and the OpenFeature providers spec section were not fetched — URLs are listed above.
- The Fowler toggle-category chart: I got a paraphrased axis placement (Ops Toggles = mostly short-lived, moderately dynamic) rather than verbatim chart labels. Treat the exact axis wording as DOĞRULANMADI; the surrounding prose quotes are solid.

## Rapor 20

All five posts retrieved in full from the live public Substack (no paywall). WebFetch's summarizer refuses verbatim reproduction, so I pulled raw HTML via curl and stripped tags — everything below is verbatim from the article body, checked against the fetched source. Dates confirmed from each page's embedded ISO timestamp.

## 1. "Defensibility & Competition" — Elad Gil, Feb 16, 2023
https://blog.eladgil.com/p/defensibility-and-competition
Subtitle: "Are early SaaS or AI companies ever defensible early? What is the basis for competition for a startup?"

**Opening / framing (verbatim):**
- "There is a lot of discussion of whether building 'a mere wrapper on top of GPT' is defensible."
- "In this post I argue most SaaS software starts off default non-defensible (hence all the HackerNews posts & Reddit threads saying as much), and tend to build a moat over time."
- Section header, verbatim: "**Most early stage startups are not very defensible**"
- "Definitionally, it is easy to copy or clone something that has taken a handful of people a handful of months to build."
- "However, the vast majority of SaaS, consumer, and certain types of AI companies are easily and rapidly cloneable. Yet many massive companies have been formed in areas that a priori may be easy to copy or build."
- "While a handful of startups start off with defensibility intact, the vast majority do not and need to add defensibility over time. There are a few forms of defensibility that tend to emerge:"

**THE ENUMERATED LIST OF DEFENSIBILITY TYPES — verbatim, in his order.** It is a bulleted list, not numbered; several items have verbatim sub-bullets. Bold labels are his own bullet openers.

- "**Network effect.** In network effects every user creates more value for other users, forming a positive feedback loop. Network effects can be local (e.g. the more people who use Slack within a company the more benefit) or global (the more people on Instagram, the more people engage across networks and geos). Few SaaS companies have true network effects, with companies like Github as a potential counter example. Uber had a network effect and positive feedback loop between driver and rider liquidity in a given city. Network effects are one of the few forms of defensibility that can arise immediately upon launch of a company."
- "**Platform effect.** Some SaaS or operating system companies benefit from a platform effect. For example, Salesforce is a sticky product because so many other companies have integrated against it. Platforms usually come after a company has been in existence for a few years and has enough users that others want to build against its platform to reach said users."
- "**Integrations.** Related to platform effects, sometimes a company becomes defensible via integrations. Integrations may include:"
  - "The company integrates against many other APIs, code bases, etc. that are hard to reproduce (see eg some forms of crypto custody such as Anchorage where each codebase and blockchain needs to be audited for security + added"
  - "Services does integrations for the company against other vendors. For example, ERP systems like SAP and Netsuite are hard to displace as each implementation is a unique, multi-quarter process to integrate the ERP system with many other vendors inside an enterprise. EMRs such as Epic are another example of integration-driven and long-term contract driven defensibility."
- "**Building a ton of stuff.**"
  - "**Bundling.** Bundling and cross selling products prevent other companies from finding a wedge to compete with you, or create better sales success via superior interoperability, pricing, or procurement processes. See Workday, Rippling, Deel or Ashby as examples of companies that early on planned to bundle multiple products usually provided by different vendors."
  - "**Big product footprint.** For some products, the breadth and depth of the product footprint means it is hard for new entrants to compete, as it takes too much time to reach feature parity before one can sell to customers competitively. This is why many startups first launch to the low end of a market segment, and then build depth of product over time as they move up into enterprise."
- "**Deals.** Different types of deals can create defensive moats. This may include:"
  - "**Early access.** Early access to APIs, data or other unique assets may provide a competitive advantage. For example, OpenAI has provided some companies with early access to GPT-4."
  - "**Exclusive provider or distribution.** Sometimes locking in as an exclusive provider can get scale, brand, or distribution advantages for a company. For example, Google's early exclusive deals to power search for Yahoo! And AOL were company-making moments. Similarly, Microsoft's early OS deal with IBM was a king making moment."
  - "**Backend.** Some markets require hard to get deals in place or ones that take a year to execute and implement. This may include things like payment bank ends or banking access."
- "**Sales as moat.** Many SaaS or enterprise companies eventually develop sales driven moats. Moats may include:"
  - "**Multi year contracts.** Long term contracts to lock in customers for multiple years. Some industries have extreme versions of this where customers sign up for 5-7 year deals, so once a market is locked up it is hard for a new entrant to gain any share."
  - "**Sales process.** Often when selling to a large enterprise you need to go through multiple reviews and functions at the buyer including security review and audit, procurement, IT, and other areas. These reviews can take many months to complete, so it is often easier for enterprises to buy a less-good bundled product from an existing vendor, then a better stand alone product from a new supplier."
- "**Regulatory.** Some companies receive regulatory approvals that provide a moat. For example, early on AngelList received a 'No Action' letter from the SEC that allowed it to run syndicates."
- "**Data or system of record effect.** Having unique or proprietary data, or owning a customers data or having a long historical record of it can create defensibility. Similarly, being a 'system of record', for a user, entity, etc. can be a powerful position to be in. **On average, data effects tend to be overstated outside of highly scaled consumer products or very specific verticals.**"
- "**Scale effects.**"
  - "**Capital scale.** Having access to large sums of money sometimes creates the environment to execute quickly and build a network effect or defensible position - this happened with both Uber and TikTok."
  - "**Business scale and negotiation.** Sometimes pre-negotiating 'scaled pricing' allows you to offer a service cheaper than anyone else, creating a self-fulfilling loop. This happened with Intel and early semiconductor releases. Similarly, there are scale effects in payments or other services, where the more volume you have, the cheaper your own backend providers become."
- "**Open source.**"
  - "**Creator advantage.** Sometimes the teams that implemented or created an open source software project are best positioned to commercialize said product, given their ability to drive and control contributions to said project + brand and relationships in that community. See e.g. dbt Labs."
  - "**Openness.** Some dev teams only want to adopt open-source software for specific areas."
- "**Brand.** Eventually a brand may be built that positions a company as the default choice for a specific use case."
- "**IP Moat.** Intellectual property tends to be more capable of protecting hard tech or biotech companies than most consumer or SaaS products."
- "**Other forms of defensibility.** Usually the forms below create the largest advantage when competing with incumbents versus other startups, as other startups may be able to copy said advantages."
  - "**Speed.** One of the few advantage a startup has against an incumbent is its speed. It definitionally will have fewer people, less money, fewer customers and a smaller product footprint. Speed of iteration and execution, responsiveness to customers request, and speed of hiring and closing candidates are all advantages. Oddly, speed also turns out to be an advantage versus most other startups - few teams can execute at high levels consistently."
  - "**Pricing.** Occasionally startups may have a pricing advantage relative incumbents (but maybe not other startups) via a lower cost structure, a lack of an existing product to cannibalize, or due to a different business model. In some cases a startup can give something away for free as a wedge to cross sell an incremental product in a way others can not compete. 'Your margin is my opportunity' is a famous quote by Jeff Bezos on Amazon and its advantages entering new markets."
  - "**New business models.** Sometimes a startup can innovate on business model to create a higher leverage business or different incentive structure. For example, Anduril in defensetech has a traditional tech margin-based business, while all the incumbents sell 'cost plus'..."

**Closing line, verbatim:** "Most of the forms of defensibility above take a few years to build."

**Incumbents vs startups (verbatim):**
- "Often in the first 4-5 years of a successful startup's life, its main competition is other startups. After a company starts to break away from the startup pack, and proves a large market, that is when a big company tends to remobilize and steer into the direction of a startup."
- "Usually this transition from startup competition to incumbent competition takes between 4-7 years."
- "In other circumstances, an incumbent may be aware of a large and obvious opportunity and steer into it much faster - we are seeing this happen now in multiple areas of generative AI where incumbents like Microsoft, Github, Notion, and Intercom are amongst the first to act."
- "Fast market entry or expansion by an incumbent can at times be the death knell for a startup, especially if done early (i.e. the first 1-3 years of startup life)."

**The "wrapper" answer (verbatim, section titled "What does this all mean for the 'wrapper on OpenAI'?"):**
- "The takeaway is that serving a customer need well is often more important (and harder) to think about than defensibility. In many cases defensibility emerges over time - particularly if you build out a proprietary data set or become an ingrained workflow, or create defensibility via sales or other moats."
- "The less building and expansion of the product you do after launch, the more vulnerable you will be to other startups or incumbents eventually coming after and commoditizing you. Pace of execution and ongoing shipping post v1 matters a lot to building one forms of defensibility above."
- "Beware the fast-acting incumbent[2] with the easy to add product space that is an obvious and necessary extension of what they are already doing."

---

## 2. "AI: Startup Vs Incumbent Value" — Elad Gil, Oct 25, 2022
https://blog.eladgil.com/p/ai-startup-vs-incumbent-value

- "In each technology wave the value, revenue, market cap, profits and great people captured by startups versus incumbents differs. In some waves it all goes to startups, while in others it goes to incumbents or is split between them. Unexpectedly, the prior wave of value from AI roughly all went to incumbents over startups, despite a lot of startup activity."
- "In the first internet wave most of the value went to startups (Google, Amazon, Paypal, Ebay, Salesforce, Facebook, Netflix) while some was captured by incumbents (Microsoft, Apple, IBM, Oracle, Adobe) who extended their franchises onto the internet. Perhaps this was a 60:40 or 70:30 startup:incumbent split."
- "For mobile, most of the value went to incumbents (Apple, Google, and then every mobile version of an incumbent's app - e.g. 'Mobile CRM' was not a stand alone startup but rather Salesforce on your iphone)... Perhaps this was a 20:80 startup:incumbent split."
- "Machine learning has been an odd one where the first wave of value (machine vision, RNNs, CNNs, early GANs, deep learning) went roughly entirely to incumbents."
- **On the 10X bar (the key line for beating incumbents):** "To beat an incumbent as a startup you usually need to either build something so dramatically better that you overcome the distribution, capital, and pre-existing product moats of the incumbent, or you need to focus on a brand new customer segment or distribution moat the incumbent can not serve for some reason. In general you need a 10X better product."
- **On data advantage:** "Many of the largest scale uses of AI to date have been at consumer-centric companies that have large data sets to train on (Google, Facebook, Uber, etc). Perhaps incumbents won due to a data advantage that is now going away as companies use the broader internet as an initial training set + are switching to models that work more robustly against smaller data sets?"
- **On bundling as incumbent weapon:** "An incumbent can be 50% as good as something, but as long as they bundle it with a core pre-existing product with lots of customers they can still win (see e.g. Teams versus Slack)."
- **On hard markets:** "Hard markets include things like education or healthcare, where technological innovation is often crushed by market structure, regulation, or a seeming indifference to actual end-user needs by people already in the field."
- "Some markets are hard, and even if adding machine learning makes something 10X better, it may not get adopted for other reasons."
- **Why this wave differs:** "There is reason to believe while incumbents should capture a good amount of the value in this wave, startups will take a bigger share of AI generated value this time around."
- "This time, the technology seems dramatically stronger, which means it is easier to create 10X better products to overcome incumbent advantages. The 'why now' may simply be a technology sea change."
- **Where apps work (verbatim list):** "In general, this wave of AI applications seems to do best in markets where: There are highly repetitive, highly paid tasks (code, marketing copy, images for websites etc) / Imperfect fidelity is fine, as you have a human in the loop who wants to review the items (which creates a nice feedback loop or future training set)... / Workflow tools do not exist or are weak for the use case, so the AI features become a core and useful part of a broader workflow tool / Summarization or generation of text or images is useful for the product application..."
- **On scale (section "Scale matters"):** "For example, a 10% increase in Google's market cap is currently $130 Billion, or the equivalent of almost 7 Figmas, 4 Snowflakes, 17 Githubs, or 130 Stability.AIs! The market caps of incumbents have gotten so large that even small changes can add up to entire ecosystems or market segments."
- "Even if incumbents capture most of the value this time due to raw scale, startups should participate in a significant way in new market cap and impact to the world."

## 3. "AI Platforms, Markets, & Open Source" — Elad Gil, Feb 15, 2023
https://blog.eladgil.com/p/ai-platforms-markets-and-open-source
(Note exact title uses "&", not "and"; URL slug says "and". Subtitle: "What does the future market structure look like for AI foundation and API companies? How does OSS play a role in a world of ever scaling models?")

- "Market structures matter a lot as it determines both the economic and talent winner in an ecosystem (who gets all the revenue, talent, profits, market cap and innovation)."
- **Oligopoly thesis:** "The reason to argue for a near term oligopoly market, versus likely fragmentation, is due to the capital/compute/data scale costs currently needed for each subsequently better performing LLM model."
- "If GPT-3 at the time cost a few million to ten million or so to train, and GPT-4 from scratch may be estimated at tens of millions to maybe a hundred million, maybe GPT-5 is a few hundred million and GPT-N is a billion."
- **Capital as moat:** "For some period of time, a strong moat will exist for foundation model companies, limiting new entrants (or forcing every startup to have a 'strategic' backer with deep financial pockets). For example if it eventually costs $500M+ to train each new model generation, not many companies will be able to afford to compete[1]."
- "The eventual capabilities of a foundation model on a single architecture should in the limit approach an asymptote, eventually removing the competitive advantage or big step function with each model/scale."
- "Older versions of models may be cheaper to retrain equivalents of, meaning fragmentation and/or open sources will be a generation behind."
- **Intel analogy:** "Each Intel CPU design & fab (on roughly an 18-month iteration timeframes - sound familiar?) cost quite a bit more to build than the prior generation. This, alongside clever sales and marketing deals, enforced a moat behind Intel in its market."
- "We may end up with a world where GPT N (or equivalent) is at the cutting edge with clear differentiation for key applications, while GPT N-1 or N-2 is cheap and broadly accessible for applications with lower fidelity needs or 'good enough' models."
- "If you assume the foundation model training costs continue to ramp with each subsequent model, then the next 2-3 years is probably the last chance for a startup to enter this market without a deep-pocketed 'strategic' backer such as Microsoft, Google, Facebook, Apple or Amazon."
- **Distribution/brand path:** "Another view of the world is that models will commoditize rapidly, but brand, distribution, and product will matter as much or more. For example Bing is roughly equivalent to Google on many measures but Google has maintained its market position via buying paid distribution + branding."
- **Open source:** "This suggests in the medium term, unless a large-scale backer of OS LLMs emerges, OS LLMs may end up a generation or two behind (1-2 years?) closed source models due to the sheer cost of training."
- "Models that are 1 generation behind will be incredibly useful for all sorts of developers and applications, but the most cutting edge highest fidelity models for the most advanced uses may stay closed source."
- **THE PLATFORM-vs-APP ANSWER (section header verbatim: "B5: Does value aggregate to platforms or apps?"):**
  - "In some worlds, much of the application value aggregates to the platform as it forward integrates into the handful of apps that do best on it. For example, Microsoft famously bought or killed all the competitors to MS Office. In contrast, AWS, Azure, and GCP are all massive businesses - and yet roughly all SaaS, enterprise, and consumer companies benefit from these platforms and have aggregated their own value without the platforms forward integrating into them."
  - "**The most likely answer on platforms versus apps is 'both'. Some platforms will forward integrate into obvious applications (ChatGpt?) while most applications will be stand-alone and be able to create enormous value of their own. Yet other applications will use bespoke niche models and proprietary data to differentiate.**"
- **On image-gen differentiation:** "Image gen may ultimately differentiate based on proprietary data sets and training approaches versus raw scale of capital/compute and data in the short term."
- **On scale bottlenecks:** "Overall, while there are a lot of reasonably orthogonal ways to scale things up that are not purely capital based. However, in the medium term it seems that even if you implement many of these things, scale will still create an advantage for LLMs."
- **Regulation/China:** "China's government has long used regulatory and literal firewalls to prevent competition from non-Chinese companies and to build local, government supported and censored champions." (this line is actually from #4 — see below; corrected there)

## 4. "Things I Don't Know About AI" — Elad Gil, Feb 21, 2024
https://blog.eladgil.com/p/things-i-dont-know-about-ai

- "In most markets, the more time passes the clearer things become. In generative AI ('AI'), it has been the opposite. The more time passes, the less I think I actually understand."
- "In 2021 I wrote that I thought the frontier models market would collapse over time into an oligopoly market due to the scale of capital needed. In parallel, non-frontier models would more commodity / pricing driven and have a stronger opensource presence."
- "Frontier models keep getting more and more expensive to train, while commodity models drop in price each year as performance goes up (for example, it is probably ~5X cheaper to train GPT-3.5 equivalent now than 2 years ago)"
- "As model scale has gotten larger, funding increasingly has been primarily coming from the cloud providers / big tech. For example, Microsoft invested $10B+ in OpenAI, while Anthropic raised $7B between Amazon and Google. ... The venture funding for these companies in contrast is a tiny drop in the ocean in comparison."
- "Are cloud providers king-making a handful of players at the frontier and locking in the oligopoly market via the sheer scale of compute/capital they provide? ... Cloud providers are easily the biggest funders of foundation models, not venture capitalists."
- **Value-shift across the stack:** "A fully open source model performing at the very frontier of AI has the potential to flip a subportion the economic share of AI infra from LLMs towards cloud and inference providers and decreases revenue away from the other LLM foundation model companies."
- **Licensing as moat:** "One of the most brilliant things in the Llama2 terms of use is the open commercial use of the license if you have fewer then 700 million users[1]. This obviously prevents some large competitors from using their models. But it also means if you are a big cloud provider you need to pay a license to Meta for Llama, which Microsoft has already done. This creates an interesting long term way for Meta to control (& monetize) Llama despite being open source."
- **Regulation / M&A:** "How does FTC (and other regulator) prevention of M&A impact this market? There are at least a dozen credible companies building AI cloud related products and services - too many for all of them to be stand alone. How does one think about exits under an administration that is aggressively against tech M&A?"
- **Government/regulatory champions:** "Do governments back (or direct their purchasing to) regional AI champions? Will national governments differentially spend on local models a la Boeing vs Airbus in aerospace?" and "China's government has long used regulatory and literal firewalls to prevent competition from non-Chinese companies and to build local, government supported and censored champions."
- **App layer / incumbents:** "B2B apps. What will be the important companies and markets in the emerging wave of B2B apps? Where will incumbents gain value versus startups? I have a long post on this coming shortly."
- "That said, why are there so few consumer builders in the AI ecosystem? Is it purely the time delay mentioned above? It seems like the 2007-2012 social product cohort has aged out."

## 5. "Early days of AI (and AI Hype Cycle)" — Elad Gil, Aug 21, 2023
https://blog.eladgil.com/p/early-days-of-ai

- "Until the rise of new AI architectures (in particular transformer-based and diffusion-model based approaches), roughly all machine learning startups failed. **Value in prior AI waves went largely to incumbents over startups - as the capabilities were not advanced enough to create new market openings.**"
- "When many business people talk about 'AI' today, they treat it as a continuum with past capabilities of the CNN/RNN/GAN world. In reality it is a step function in new capabilities and products enabled, and marks the dawn of a new era of tech."
- "It is almost like cars existed, and someone invented an airplane and said 'an airplane is just another kind of car - but with wings' - instead of mentioning all the new use cases and impact to travel, logistics, defense, and other areas. The era of aviation would have kicked off, not the 'era of even faster cars'."
- **Enterprise timing:** "Given that large enterprise planning cycles often take 3-6 months, and then prototyping and building will take a year for a large company, we are still very far away from peak AI usage or peak AI hype. Most large enterprises are still trying to analytically sort what 'AI' means for them, and are still many quarters from embracing this new technology."
- **Wave 2 (startups vs fast incumbents):** "This is the first wave of startups to launch on top of GPT-3.5/4 like Perplexity, Langchain, Harvey or others. In parallel, a small number of founder led multi-billion companies like Navan, Notion, Quora, Replit, and Zapier launched AI-powered products quickly and are the early adopters of the wave. Microsoft, Adobe, and Google are notable outliers as very large enterprise moving fast to AI - Microsoft likely due to its inside track with OpenAI..."
- **Wave 4:** "Since enterprise planning and build cycles are so long, anticipate the first really products (versus demos or prototypes) from larger companies other than MSFT, Adobe, Google, Meta to start to show up in a year or two. This is when revenue to AI infra companies will start to ramp significantly relative to today, when hype will peak..."
- "It is the very earliest days of AI, and both peak hype and peak impact are still in the future."

## DÖKÜM — what else I saw / what I could not verify

- **Titles differ from your brief.** #1 is "Defensibility & Competition" (ampersand). #3 is "AI Platforms, Markets, & Open Source" — the URL slug says "and". #5 is "Early days of AI (and AI Hype Cycle)", not "Early Days of AI". If Damla cites these anywhere, use the exact forms above.
- **#5 is the weakest source for your moat question.** It has almost nothing on defensibility, switching costs, or network effects. Its one usable moat-adjacent line is the "value in prior AI waves went largely to incumbents" opener. Don't over-lean on it.
- **The word "moat" never appears in #4 or #5.** In #4 the whole piece is literally a question list — nearly every sentence ends in "?". If you quote #4, quote it as open questions, not as claims. Misquoting his questions as assertions would be an easy factual error.
- **#3 contains a self-contradiction Gil flags himself:** he predicted foundation-model capital moats, then #4 (a year later) says the moat shifted to cloud providers as funders. That is a useful "he updated" arc if you want a narrative.
- **Nobody says "wrapper is fine" outright.** #1's actual position is narrower: defensibility is *usually absent at launch and built over 2-5 years*, and the risk is not the wrapper itself but *stopping shipping* ("The less building and expansion of the product you do after launch, the more vulnerable you will be"). If Damla wants to cite Gil against "wrapper = flop", the honest reading is "wrapper at v1 is normal; wrapper at v5 is dead."
- **His data-moat line is a downgrade, not an upgrade:** "On average, data effects tend to be overstated outside of highly scaled consumer products or very specific verticals." This cuts against the common "our proprietary data is our moat" pitch. Worth knowing before it goes into a deck.
- **"Switching costs" as a phrase appears nowhere.** His nearest equivalents are "Sales as moat" (multi-year contracts, procurement), "Integrations", and "Data or system of record effect." Don't attribute the phrase to him.
- **Typos are his, preserved verbatim above** ("bank ends" for back ends, "fewer then", "did no exist", "white color jobs", "wild care"). Do not silently fix them inside quotation marks — quote-and-[sic] or paraphrase around them.
- **Not accessed / not checked:** the two footnote-linked prior posts he references ("AI Revolution – Transformers and Large Language Models", "2023 Unicorn Market Cap & Rise of AI", "AI Regulation"). #4 promises "I have a long post on this coming shortly" about B2B apps and incumbents — that follow-up post exists somewhere but I did not locate it. If the incumbents-vs-startups-in-B2B angle matters, that's the missing piece.
- **Images/charts in #3 and #5** (the 2x2 model segmentation grid, the Ballinger slide, the "slide I now use") are not recoverable as text — only their captions. Their content is **DOĞRULANMADI**.
- No files written.

# Stack Overflow Developer Survey — AI usage & trust, 2023–2026

## 1. AI tool usage in the development process

| Year | Currently using | Planning to use soon | Not planning | Total using-or-planning |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2023 | **43.78%** | **25.46%** | **29.4%** | ~70% |
| 2024 | **62%** | ~14% (derived) | ~24% (derived) | **76%** |
| 2025 | not published as a separate split (see note) | — | — | **84%** |
| 2026 | no results yet | — | — | — |

- 2023 figures: VERIFIED, primary — https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2023/ (AI section). The three buckets are given to two decimals on the survey page itself.
- 2024: "76% of all respondents are using or are planning to use AI tools in their development process this year" and "62%" currently using — VERIFIED, primary — https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2024/ai. The 14% planning / 24% not-planning are DERIVED BY SUBTRACTION, not printed on the page.
- 2025: "84% of respondents are using or planning to use AI tools in the development process," up from 76% — VERIFIED, primary — https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2025/ai. Also primary: **51% of professional developers use AI tools daily**.
- UNVERIFIED / CONFLICT: one Stack Overflow blog summary rendered the 2025 headline as "**80% of developers now using AI tools**" (https://stackoverflow.blog/2025/12/29/developers-remain-willing-but-reluctant-to-use-ai-the-2025-developer-survey-results-are-here). I could not locate an explicit "currently using" number on the primary 2025 AI page to confirm or reject 80%. Do not state a 2025 currently-using figure without re-checking.

## 2. Trust / distrust in the accuracy of AI output

**2023** — VERIFIED, primary (https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2023/), full 5-point scale:
- Highly trust **2.85%**
- Somewhat trust **39.3%**
- Neither **30.68%**
- Somewhat distrust **21.71%**
- Highly distrust **5.46%**
→ Trust total ≈ **42.2%**, distrust total ≈ **27.2%**.

**2024** — VERIFIED, primary (https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2024/ai):
- "**43%** feel good about AI accuracy" (trust)
- "**31%** are skeptical" — broken out on the page as **23% somewhat distrust + 8% highly distrust**
- Neutral ≈ 26% (derived)
- Related: **45%** of professional developers rate AI tools "bad or very bad" at handling complex tasks.
- The 2024 press release framed this as the use/trust gap widening: https://stackoverflow.co/company/press/archive/stack-overflow-2024-developer-survey-gap-between-ai-use-trust/ (UNVERIFIED — press domain blocked, seen only via search snippet).

**2025** — VERIFIED, primary (https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2025/ai), verbatim:
> "More developers actively distrust the accuracy of AI tools (**46%**) than trust it (**33%**), and only a fraction (**3%**) report 'highly trusting' the output."
- Among experienced developers: **2.6%** highly trust, **20%** highly distrust.
- Related: the #1 reason developers would still ask a human is "when I don't trust AI's answers" — **75%**.

**Your premise checks out, with one correction.** Trust did decline: ~42% (2023) → 43% (2024) → 33% (2025); active distrust rose 31% (2024) → 46% (2025). But note the trust line was roughly *flat* 2023→2024 and only fell in 2025 — it is not a three-year slide.

**CONFLICT — flag this before quoting.** A competing figure of "**29%** trust AI output to be accurate, down from **40%** in 2024" appears in the Stack Overflow blog write-up, the press release, and downstream coverage (ADTmag, byteiota). The primary survey page says 33% and 46%. Both pairs (33/46 and 29/40) circulate under Stack Overflow's own name. Most likely these are different cuts — e.g. all respondents vs. professional developers only — but I could NOT confirm which, because the pages that would disambiguate went unreachable. **Use 33% / 46% (primary site) and do not cite 29% / 40% publicly without resolving this.**

## 3. The 2025 headline frustration stats

- "**AI solutions that are almost right, but not quite**" — the single biggest frustration, **66%**. VERIFIED, primary — https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2025/ai.
- "Debugging AI-generated code is more time-consuming" — **45%**, the second-biggest. VERIFIED, primary, same page.
- **~35%** of visits to Stack Overflow are the result of an AI-related issue at least some of the time. **PARTIALLY VERIFIED** — this appears in the Stack Overflow blog (https://stackoverflow.blog/2025/12/29/developers-remain-willing-but-reluctant-to-use-ai-the-2025-developer-survey-results-are-here) and in Stack Overflow's own press release, and in the primary 2025 site's overview text as retrieved via proxy ("approximately 35% of developers report visiting Stack Overflow for AI-related issues at least occasionally"). I could not re-open the specific primary page to quote it verbatim. Treat the number as solid, the exact wording as not nailed down.

One caution: one blog-summary retrieval swapped the 66% and 45% labels (claiming 45% for "almost right" and 66% for time spent fixing). The primary site ordering — **66% "almost right, but not quite"**, **45% debugging time** — is the correct one.

## 4. Favorability toward AI tools

| Year | Favorable / very favorable |
|---|---|
| 2023 | **77%** (27.72% very favorable + 48.56% favorable = 76.28%, rounded to 77% on the page) |
| 2024 | **72%** |
| 2025 | **60%** |

- 2023: VERIFIED, primary — https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2023/. Full scale: very favorable 27.72%, favorable 48.56%, indifferent 16.5%, unsure 4.02%, unfavorable 2.76%, very unfavorable 0.44%.
- 2024: VERIFIED, primary — https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2024/ai ("72% of all respondents are favorable or very favorable of AI tools").
- 2025: VERIFIED, primary — https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2025/ai. Positive sentiment fell from 70%+ in 2023 and 2024 to **60%**. Splits: professional developers **61%** favorable; people learning to code **53%** favorable.

Your 77 / 72 / 60 sequence is correct as stated.

## 5. Sample sizes and dates

| Year | Respondents | Countries | Field dates | Results published |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2023 | **89,184** | — | **May 8–19, 2023** | 2023 |
| 2024 | **65,437** | **185** | **May 19 – June 20, 2024** | see note |
| 2025 | **49,009** | **177** | **May 29 – June 23, 2025** | **Dec 29, 2025** |
| 2026 | fielding closed / results pending | — | opened **June 23, 2026** | not yet |

- 2023: VERIFIED, primary — https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2023/. (Note: the survey page's own "over 90,000 responses" phrasing appears in press coverage; the methodology count is 89,184.)
- 2024: VERIFIED, primary — https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2024/methodology. ~20,000 responses excluded by qualification screening; 48,019 used for salary data; median completion ~21 minutes.
- 2025: VERIFIED, primary — https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2025/methodology. 49,009 responses from 177 countries; ~15,000 excluded in quality screening; Reddit ads accounted for <2% of responses. Methodology change: multi-select answers now reported as % of unique respondents rather than raw counts — **this breaks strict year-over-year comparability on multi-select questions**, which matters if you cite trend lines.
- Publication date Dec 29, 2025 is from the blog URL/post itself.
- 2026: the survey **is open but results are not published**. Announcement: https://stackoverflow.blog/2026/06/23/the-2026-developer-survey-is-now-open-for-human-developers-only/ — "for human developers only." It teases that "agent usage has doubled since then, but the concerns they have about AI code have grown as well," and frames the AI arc as "from YOLO to ROI."

**Important trap:** several outlets (byteiota, Cadence blog) publish articles titled "Stack Overflow Dev Survey **2026**" that recycle the 2025 survey's numbers (84% / 3% / 46%), presumably because results dropped in late Dec 2025. There is no 2026 dataset. If you see an "84% adoption, 3% highly trust" stat labelled 2026, it is the 2025 survey.

# Dump — things I found that you didn't ask for

**Stack Overflow's own usage / relevance (directly relevant to your framing):**
- Stack Overflow remains the **#1 developer community platform at 84% usage**, ahead of GitHub (67%) and YouTube (61%) — 2025. UNVERIFIED (from the blog summary retrieval, not confirmed on the primary page). Note the collision: 84% is also the AI-adoption headline number. Do not conflate them.
- The ~35% AI-related-visit stat is Stack Overflow arguing that AI failure is now a *driver of traffic to Stack Overflow*, not just a competitor. That is the load-bearing narrative of the whole 2025 release.
- Stack Overflow published a follow-up specifically on this: "Mind the gap: Closing the AI trust gap for developers," https://stackoverflow.blog/2026/02/18/closing-the-developer-ai-trust-gap/ (Feb 18, 2026) — NOT FETCHED, domain blocked. Likely the most quotable company-position piece if you can open it.
- "What leaders need to know from the 2025 Stack Overflow Developer Survey," https://stackoverflow.blog/2025/10/23/what-leaders-need-to-know-from-the-2025-stack-overflow-developer-survey/ — NOT FETCHED. Date (Oct 23, 2025) predates the Dec 29 full-results post, so it may cover a partial/early release.

**AI agents (2025) — all primary, https://survey.stackoverflow.co/2025/ai:**
- **52%** of developers either don't use agents at all or stick to simpler AI tools; **38%** have no plans to adopt agents.
- **52%** agree AI tools/agents had a positive effect on their productivity.
- Among agent users, **84%** use them for software development specifically.
- UNVERIFIED (blog summary): 52% say agents affected how they complete work; 69% report increased personal productivity from agents. These conflict in shape with the 52% figure above — the blog retrieval was unreliable on numbers, so treat as suspect.

**Developer sentiment, learning, jobs (2025) — UNVERIFIED, blog summary retrieval only:**
- **72%** do not practice "vibe coding" as part of professional work; ~5% actively reject it.
- **64%** do not see AI as a threat to their job — *down* from 68% the prior year.
- **69%** learned a new coding technique or language in the past year; **44%** used AI-enabled tools to learn, up from 37% in 2024.
- ~36% learned to code specifically for AI applications.

**Trend note worth having:** the two-year arc is adoption up (70 → 76 → 84) while favorability falls (77 → 72 → 60) and distrust nearly doubles (27 → 31 → 46). Adoption and sentiment decoupled in 2025 specifically — 2024 was the year adoption jumped hardest (44 → 62 currently using, an 18-point move) with sentiment barely moving. That decoupling is the actual story, and it's stronger than a flat "trust is declining" line.

# What I could NOT access
- **Direct WebFetch to `survey.stackoverflow.co`, `stackoverflow.blog`, and `stackoverflow.co` — all blocked** by this environment's domain-safety check. Everything primary above came via the `r.jina.ai` text proxy, which then also became blocked mid-research. The percentages are the real page text, but I could not do a final verbatim re-read of several pages.
- **2024 full 5-point trust scale.** Only the 43% / 31% (23+8) aggregates are printed; the granular buckets (highly trust, somewhat trust, neither) were not retrievable and may not be published for 2024 the way they were for 2023.
- **2024 full favorability breakdown** (very favorable vs favorable etc.) — only the 72% aggregate.
- **2025 "currently using" vs "planning to use" split** — never located. Only the 84% combined and 51% daily-use figures are confirmed.
- **The 33% vs 29% trust discrepancy** — unresolved, as flagged above. This is the one number in your list I would not publish without another pass.
- **2025 press release** (https://stackoverflow.co/company/press/archive/stack-overflow-2025-developer-survey/, titled "Trust in AI at an All Time Low") — blocked, seen only through search snippets.
- **The three stackoverflow.blog analysis posts** (Oct 2025 leaders post, Feb 2026 trust-gap post, and a verbatim read of the Dec 2025 results post) — blocked.
- **2026 survey close date and expected results date** — not stated in the announcement post.

# 1. THE ANNUAL PRESENTATIONS

Index page: **https://www.ben-evans.com/presentations** — "Every year, I produce a big presentation exploring macro and strategic trends in the tech industry." Five decks are live:

| Deck | Title (title slide, verbatim) | Date (title slide) | URL |
|---|---|---|---|
| 2023 | "AI, and everything else" | December 2023 | https://www.ben-evans.com/s/2024-AI-and-Everything-Else-11.pdf |
| 2024 | "AI eats the world" | November 2024 | https://www.ben-evans.com/s/AI-eats-the-world.pdf |
| 2025 Spring | "AI eats the world" | May 2025 | https://www.ben-evans.com/s/2025-Spring-AI.pdf |
| 2025 Autumn | "AI eats the world" | November 2025 | https://www.ben-evans.com/s/2025-Autumn-AI.pdf |
| 2026 Spring | "AI eats the world" | May 2026 | https://www.ben-evans.com/s/2026-Spring-AI.pdf |

**Note:** the deck is now twice-yearly ("Spring"/"Autumn"), not annual. There is a 2026 edition (May 2026) — it is the current one. No autumn-2026 deck exists yet as of 18 Aug 2026.

All quotes below are verbatim slide text extracted with `pdftotext` from the PDFs themselves (title line = slide headline, second line = slide subhead).

### 2023 deck — "AI, and everything else" (December 2023)
No use of the word "moat" anywhere in this deck. The value-capture frame is the platform-shift template:

> **"How is value captured in a platform shift?"** / "If generative AI is the new platform - this is the pattern for how new platforms work"
> Three columns: **"Incumbents make it a feature"** · **"Startups use it to unbundle"** · **"Sometimes, you change the nature of the market"**

> "There are two ways to make money. You can bundle, or you can unbundle" — Jim Barksdale *(quoted in every deck from 2023 to 2025 Autumn)*

> **"Unbundling"** / "How do you turn a blank canvas into solutions? But then, how do those solutions get unbundled into new companies?"

> "Every Unix function became a company / Every Excel template became a company / Every Notion template… / Every ChatGPT suggestion…"

> **"Bundling or unbundling?"** / "Do LLMs mean all these apps are swallowed by a few big models - or do they enable an explosion of new apps?"

> **"Return of the 'agent' (or the mainframe)?"** / "The 30-year dream of the general purpose 'AI Agent' that can do anything for you"

### 2024 deck — "AI eats the world" (November 2024)
> **"We have no moat"** / "Internal Google memo, May 2023…" *(source credited: SemiAnalysis)*

> **"If the moat is capital…"** / "Nvidia can't keep up with demand - for now (but semiconductors are a cyclical industry)"

> **"The 'feeds & speeds' phase of the market"** / "Many more models, many more specs to measure, and all converging on a commodity"

> "Everyone in tech is giving someone else's business model away for free" / "Meta's open source" / **"Turn models into commodity infrastructure!"**

> "Everyone in tech is giving someone else's business model away for free" / "Apple's edge computing" / **"Turn models into just another API call!"**

> "Semiconductors are cyclical / **Commodity tech goes to marginal cost** / And every new tech produces a bubble"

> "SaaS enabled a huge expansion in automation, unbundling workflows out of SAP, Excel or email"

> "New use cases, new unbundlers" / "How many AI startup are really a bet on unbundling both Oracle and ChatGPT?"

### 2025 Spring deck — "AI eats the world" (May 2025)
> **"Two years of the model wars"** / **"Lots of investment, lots of progress, no apparent defensibility"**
> Columns: "Much more capital / Pricing collapse" · "Lots of new stuff - agents, multimodal, 'reasoning', MCP etc" · **"No obvious moats / Open Source"**

> **"Models themselves seem to be commodities"** / "No apparent barriers to matching SOTA, and OpenAI's first-mover lead has steadily eroded"

> **"Product, brand or distribution?"** / **"The models may be commodities, but ChatGPT is the only 'AI' brand with consumer awareness"**

> **"The Nvidia moat"** / "Nvidia tries to build a new Sun with custom compute and a software ecosystem. (This is also a TSMC chart)"

> **"'Big Tech' strategies: "how do we use this?""** / "Incumbents always try to absorb the new thing into their existing strategies" — four columns: "Build a hardware platform (Nvidia)" · "Make it a commodity (Amazon, Meta)" · "Sell the APIs (Amazon, Google, Microsoft)" · "Make it a feature (Google, Meta, Microsoft, Apple)"

### 2025 Autumn deck — "AI eats the world" (November 2025)
This is the most moat-dense deck.

> **"Yes, but where has all this money got us?"** / "After three years, lots more science and engineering, but no real clarity on the shape of the market"
> Columns: "Models still improving / Far more models, China, OSS / Lots of new acronyms" · **"No apparent moats"** · **"No clarity on product or value capture"**

> **"Rational actors?"** / "What would you do if your company was sitting on a bubble?" — row for OpenAI: **"OpenAI has mindshare and expensive stock, but no platform, infra or moat"** → "Swap your paper for hard assets and market position"

> **"Tech versus brand versus distribution?"** / **"The models may be close to commodities (especially for general use), but market position is not, so far"**

> **"So where does a model lab compete?"** / "Where is the value capture for a research-heavy, capital-intensive commodity?"
> Three columns: "Go down the stack / Win on scale? (see: aircraft, AWS, chips)" · **"If the models are commodities? And don't have network effects?"** · "Go up the stack / Win on network effects & product? (See: software)"

> **"Where is the value capture?"** / **"If models are near-commodities, and we don't know the right product, where will the value be?"**
> Four candidate answers on one slide: **"Best model? Most capital?"** · **"Proprietary vertical data?"** · **"Distribution & GTM? Product? UX?"** · **"Building 'normal' software companies?"**

> **"Microsoft's shift away from network effects?"** / "From competing on network effects to competing on access to capital?"

> **"Startups exist to unbundle use cases"** / "The coming wave of AI startups trying to unbundle Google, Excel, email and Oracle… and ChatGPT"

> **"OpenAI bundles and unbundles use cases"** / "What's the right experience? The right distribution? And why isn't it just the ChatGPT app?"

> **"'What's our AI strategy?' Well, what's the pattern?"** / "How do we always deploy new technologies?" — "Absorb (Automate obvious use-cases / Make it a feature)" · "Innovate (New products, bundling and unbundling)" · "Disrupt (Redefine the question)". Next slide: **"So far, most successful use-cases are 'absorb'"**

> **"Where's the human in the loop?"** / "All recommendation systems today work by driving, capturing and analysing user activity" — "Pre-internet: Human editors, for both retail & media / **Physical assets as moat**" · "Internet: All of us are mechanical turks feeding algorithms / **Network effects as moat**" · "GenAI?: Can an LLM do this better? **Can an LLM do it without needing a user base?**"

> **"What do 'automated interns' change?"** / "The Jevons paradox - applied price elasticity" — includes **"Was employing lots of people your moat?"**

> **"Not everything works? Welcome to tech"** / "'Why did our AI pilot fail?' That's a CTO question, not an AI question" — "Security, privacy, IPR, error rates, legal" · "Data integration & legacy systems" · "Finding the right solution for the right people" · "The same issues as deploying any new tech"

### 2026 Spring deck — "AI eats the world" (May 2026)
> **"Commodity infra rarely captures value up the stack"** / **"Mobile networks are a trillion dollar industry, but all the use-cases and value-capture are built by other people"**

> **"And so far, models do seem to be commodities"** / **"For most general and consumer use, models are very similar, and crucially there are no network effects"**

> **"What will equilibrium look like?"** / "What will you get if you win the capex war?"
> Two columns compared — "Software: Capital-light / Network effects / Monopolies / High margin" vs "LLMs: Capital-intensive / **No network effects?** / **Commodity?** / **Low margin?**" — and the question: "**Will LLMs have pricing power? Can they capture use-cases & value further up the stack?**"

> **"A provisional thesis"** / "This is all very early, and these might be the wrong answers, but hopefully they're the right questions"
> Four boxes verbatim: **"Chat is a terrible UX / General use needs 'apps'"** · **"Labs can't build (or generate) all the apps"** · **"Models are commodities and have no network effects"** · **"Models will just be infra / Innovation will move up the stack"**

> **"Writing code isn't the hard part"** / "What should the code be doing, and where does it fit into the market? What gets bundled and unbundled?"

> **"How do you build 'AI software'?"** / "How do you turn an amazing raw technology into something people can use?"
> Middle column verbatim: **"Use cases! Tooling, GUI, process, GTM, proprietary data, workflows"** — with the closing question **"Does the model go at the top of the stack or the bottom?"**

> **"What does AI do to software? More! But then what?"** / "The best answer to this question might be 'BREATHE'" — "Coding is much faster & cheaper" · "New margin structures & competitive threats" · **"Chatbots unbundle some use-cases"** · "Do models replace software? Or make more software?"

> **"Was the cost of that task your moat?"** / **"The internet removed physical distribution costs that protected many industries from competition"**

> **"What does automation mean for you? It depends"** — includes **"Was that cost base your moat?"**

> **"So what does automation break apart now?"** / **"Which industries were protected by a cost base that AI can now automate to zero?"**

> Closing slide: **"So what can we know?"** / "We don't know how this will work, but we can try to ask the right questions" — "What can you and your competitors do with this?" · "Can this unlock/break something crucial in your business model?" · **"Presume radical uncertainty"**
